# UNCHARTED HORIZONS — Complete Tech Tree Design Document (v3.123)

## 1. System Overview

### 1.1 Core Concept
Players do not research specific technologies. Instead, they assign research effort to **Focus Areas** — broad fields of inquiry such as *Fire*, *Farming*, or *Electricity*. Over time, investment in a Focus Area produces **Breakthroughs**: specific discoveries that grant technologies, units, buildings, or abilities. Some Breakthroughs unlock new Focus Areas.

### 1.2 Discovery Mechanics

*The discovery model, written in v3.120. Every measurement since 1 October 2026 rests on it, and the design simulator implements it, except environmental prerequisites, the environment's weighting of the spread, Competing Solutions' lock-outs, UNIQUE's first-come rule and stacking penalty, theoretical revelation, necessity bonuses and the per-city modifier. Values marked provisional are playtesting levers.*

**In brief.** A civilisation's Insight is its science, routed to Focus Areas. A breakthrough is found in two steps. First its **idea must occur**: someone in the civilisation has the insight that makes study possible. CORE ideas occur at once; for every other tier, occurring is luck, a chance each turn during a limited moment, and the tier sets the odds. Then the breakthrough is **studied**: its focus's Insight fills a hidden meter, priced by its era and by how far it lies ahead of the civilisation's development. Funding decides how fast study goes; luck decides which ideas come, and when.

**The active focus and the spread:**
A civilisation directs its research at **one Focus Area at a time**, the *active focus*, which receives **80%** of each turn's Insight (**50%** before Specialisation of Labour, below). Switching focus is a deliberate strategic choice, not a per-turn slider allocation: each turn's commitment matters, and the resulting technology profile is characterfully uneven rather than uniformly broad. A Grand Endeavour (Section 1.5), while active, takes the active focus's share.

The rest is the **spread**. It goes only to the other foci that have something under study, in proportion to the work waiting in each: every breakthrough under study counts, and counts for more the further development has moved past the start of its era, so older work draws more. The civilisation's environmental modifiers (Section 1.3) multiply each focus's draw, so a coastal civilisation's spread tilts toward Seafaring and Trade and a highland one's toward Mining and Observation. A focus with nothing under study receives nothing: no Insight drains into fields where nothing can yet be studied. Neglected fields still move, and serendipity lives in the spread (Section 1.4).

**Spill-over.** Insight a focus cannot use this turn, because it has nothing under study or has just completed all of it, goes at once to the foci that still have open work, in the same proportions. Almost nothing is lost (0.1–0.5% of Insight in the simulator).

*Specialisation of Labour.* Before SO05 the split is a gentle **50/50**: the civilisation can always lean somewhere, but cannot yet concentrate sharply. Discovering **SO05 (Specialisation of Labor)** moves it to **80/20**, roughly doubling the concentrating power of the active focus. This gives the mechanic a thematic milestone (a society that has not yet specialised its labour cannot direct its inquiry sharply) without ever creating a dead-agency window: even at 50/50 the player is always steering. SO05 is gated on a population-5 city, which on standard speed lands around **turn 25–40**, just as the tree begins to branch, which is when sharper concentration starts to matter. The gate scales with game speed automatically (Unciv applies the game-speed modifier to city growth). *(A "hard" variant, with no spread before SO05, was considered and rejected: with the prioritisation unlock itself un-prioritisable, a slow food start could leave the opening inert. It remains available as a tuning knob.)*

*Pace.* Up to the Early Modern era, §2's spans and prices give every benchmark player one turn per discovery or more, counting knowledge from rivals (v3.108; the Ancient era made dearer in v3.123). From the Modern era the prices are pinned by the science victory's length, which must match base Civ V's (§2), and discoveries come faster: about 1.1–1.4 a turn on a good economy counting rivals' knowledge, about one a turn of its own, and about one in all on an average economy. CORE ideas occur at once, so the opening's cadence is set by Insight and prices alone.

A **focus queue** supports all of the above: the player may pre-set an order of Focus Areas that auto-advances when the active focus has nothing left under study, so sequencing through the foundations never requires per-turn clicking.

**Insight, development and price:**
Insight is generated on the **same scale as base-game science**, a 1:1 mapping, not a separate currency. A civilisation's per-turn science output (from population, buildings, specialists, trade routes, era scaling) *is* its per-turn Insight, routed to Focus Areas by the split above rather than spent on a single chosen technology. This inherits Unciv's balanced science curve across the whole game, and gives prices their anchor: §2's era spans are drawn against base Civ V's.

*Development* is a civilisation's cumulative Insight placed on §2's era spans: the index of the era its total lies in, plus the fraction of that span accumulated (0 at the start of the Primitive era, 8 at the end of the Space era). It measures how far through history a civilisation's research has come, whatever it has found. (Era *entry*, §2, is a separate count of breakthroughs.)

Every breakthrough has a **price**: its era's price per discovery (§2) × 0.83 (the model's calibration, provisional) × **4 for every era it lies ahead of the civilisation's development**, the premium of being ahead of one's time. "Ahead" is counted from the start of the breakthrough's era, so a breakthrough of the next era costs 4× at the start of the current era, 2× halfway through it, and its plain price once development reaches its era. A breakthrough behind development keeps its own era's price, so catching up is fast, as in base Civ V. The premium makes early arrivals possible but expensive: ×4 per era is the steepness at which arrivals a full era early disappear, while a specialist still earns a real head start (about half an era for a Mathematics specialist's Calculus).

Two implementation points follow from the 1:1 mapping:

- **Progress is stored as a floating-point value, not an integer.** Base Unciv accumulates research toward a single tech in whole numbers (`techsInProgress: HashMap<String, Int>`), which is fine when all science funds one target. Here a breakthrough may receive a small fraction of a small spread, which would round to zero under integer storage. Since the accumulation loop is being rewritten regardless, progress uses a float so small shares still add up.
- **The per-city cost modifier is retained, applied to the price.** Base Unciv multiplies each tech's cost by `1 + (cities − 1) × techCostPerCityModifier` (`TechManager.costOfTech`), the vanilla mechanism that stops wide empires out-researching tall ones. The same multiplier applies to every breakthrough's price, preserving the vanilla tall-versus-wide balance. (The vanilla catch-up modifier `getScienceModifier` is *not* carried over: Knowledge Diffusion replaces it.)

**Ideas and their moment:**
A breakthrough becomes **discoverable** when its prerequisite breakthroughs are discovered (each prerequisite group satisfied by at least one option) and its environmental prerequisites are met. A discovery takes effect at the end of its turn, so a chain advances at most one link a turn.

A discoverable breakthrough receives no Insight at all, however hard its focus is funded, until its **idea occurs**: the moment someone in the civilisation has it. From then it is **under study**: it takes a share of its focus's Insight every turn until it is discovered, and it is never lost. Occurring is hidden; what the player sees is governed by revelation (Section 1.4).

- **CORE** ideas occur the turn the breakthrough becomes discoverable.
- **Every other idea** has a constant chance each turn through a **window**, its moment, which opens when the breakthrough becomes discoverable: half its era's length for STANDARD ideas, the whole era's length for UNCOMMON, RARE and UNIQUE ones. The chance is sized so that the odds of the idea occurring within its window are the tier's (the table below).
- **Catch-up.** Behind history the window shrinks: once the idea's era has ended, the window is divided by 1 + 4 × the eras since it ended, packing the same odds into fewer turns. A Medieval STANDARD idea is 10.0% a turn over 18 turns on time, 27% a turn over 6 turns half an era late, and 41% a turn over 4 turns a full era late, so a landlocked civilisation that reaches the coast late has its Caravel within about six turns.
- **A missed idea.** If the window passes without the idea occurring, the idea is lost. UNCOMMON, RARE and UNIQUE ideas are also lost once development is more than half an era past the end of their era, window or not. A lost idea can still return in three ways:
  - **Rediscovery on demand** (STANDARD ideas; v3.120). A lost STANDARD idea comes back when something waits on it. Each turn it has a 10% chance of occurring again for each thing waiting on it (19% with two, 27% with three): a breakthrough it alone now blocks, an FTL prototype it stands in the way of, or an open question on the FTL screen it would answer. It then goes under study at its normal price. History's version is multiple discovery (Newton and Leibniz, Darwin and Wallace): ideas that much depends on keep turning up. An idea nothing waits on stays lost, which is the variance between games the tier exists for, and UNCOMMON, RARE and UNIQUE ideas keep their odds. No game is lost to an unlucky STANDARD idea: none of 2,700 went unfinished in the measurement that adopted it (three worlds, v3.120), nor any of 900 isolated games in the discovery workshop's (14 October).
  - **Knowledge diffusion** (below) brings common knowledge from contacted civilisations.
  - **A Grand Endeavour** (Section 1.5) can complete a lost idea that has been Revealed.
- **Glimpses.** Each turn a discoverable idea fails to occur, it may be glimpsed instead: Revealed, so its goal is known, without the idea itself (Section 1.4). The chance is 10% a turn for STANDARD ideas and 3% for UNCOMMON ones (both provisional, to be calibrated), and never for RARE or UNIQUE ideas, so the surprises stay surprises.

Windows and chances by era, at a good economy's era lengths (§2; from the simulator):

| Era (turns) | STANDARD: window, chance a turn | UNCOMMON | RARE | UNIQUE (window: the era) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Primitive (33.0) | 16.5 turns, 10.9% | 1.54% | 0.32% | 0.20% |
| Ancient (36.0) | 18.0, 10.0% | 1.41% | 0.29% | 0.19% |
| Classical (28.0) | 14.0, 12.7% | 1.81% | 0.38% | 0.24% |
| Medieval (36.0) | 18.0, 10.0% | 1.41% | 0.29% | 0.19% |
| Early Modern (49.0) | 24.5, 7.5% | 1.04% | 0.21% | 0.14% |
| Modern (46.0) | 23.0, 7.9% | 1.10% | 0.23% | 0.15% |
| Atomic (36.0) | 18.0, 10.0% | 1.41% | 0.29% | 0.19% |
| Space (56.0) | 28.0, 6.6% | 0.91% | 0.19% | 0.12% |

Windows are counted in turns, so a slower economy gets the same turns and less Insight in them. (UNIQUE's figures are the simulator's flat 6.5%, which stands in for the stacking penalty below.)

**Study:**
Each breakthrough under study has a hidden **meter**, drawn when it comes under study: its price × a random factor (mean 1; the middle half of draws fall between 0.63 and 1.28) × any cost multiplier (below). A focus's Insight is shared among its breakthroughs under study in proportion to their **progress + 0.1**, progress being the Insight it has received measured in its price (0.5 once it has had half its price, whatever its hidden meter). This is *momentum*: lines already under way draw more, so arrivals stagger rather than all maturing together. Every tier is studied alike once its idea has occurred (decided 14 October 2026): rarer ideas pay for their rarity in occurring, and gain nothing by being slower to finish. Insight becomes progress at the price in force when it is spent, so a breakthrough studied ahead of its time grows cheaper as development catches up. A breakthrough whose share would complete it takes only what it needs and is discovered at the end of the turn; the remainder is shared again among the rest. Progress persists: discovering one thing never sets another back, it frees attention.

*A worked example.* A focus receives 100 Insight a turn and has three breakthroughs under study, each priced at 200 with a meter of exactly 1: A is 60% done, B 10%, C new.

| Turn | Weights (A / B / C) | Shares of 100 | After the turn |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | 0.7 / 0.2 / 0.1 | 70 / 20 / 10 | A 95%, B 20%, C 5% |
| 2 | 1.05 / 0.3 / 0.15 | 70 / 20 / 10, but A needs only 10 and is discovered; the 60 left over is shared between B and C (weights 0.3 / 0.15): 40 / 20 | A discovered; B 50%, C 20% |

So B, already under way, becomes the leading line; C keeps a small share and grows into the next one.

**Breakthrough Tiers:**

| Tier | Its idea | Odds of occurring in its moment | Design Intent |
|---|---|---|---|
| **CORE** | Occurs as soon as the breakthrough is discoverable | Certain | Foundational discoveries. Every civilisation will discover these through normal play. A player funding the right Focus Area will always get these eventually. |
| **STANDARD** | A window of half its era | 85% | Historical discoveries — things that happened in our world. Most civilisations will discover most of these. May have **environmental prerequisites** (e.g. coastal access for seafaring, iron deposits for iron smelting). A missed STANDARD idea comes back if anything waits on it. |
| **UNCOMMON** | A window of its era | 40% | Moderately hard: discoveries that turned up occasionally in history because they were difficult — not because something better pre-empted them, which is a Competing Solution (below). May have **environmental prerequisites**. A decent number of civs will discover some UNCOMMON breakthroughs, but not every civ will discover every one. |
| **RARE** | A window of its era | 10% | Speculative or alternative-history breakthroughs — things that *could* have happened but usually didn't. Won't show up every game. When they do, unlikely to be discovered by more than one or two civs. Should be exciting: powerful benefits or entirely new play styles. But the game is always completable without them. When a RARE breakthrough gates a chain of related discoveries, the follow-ups in that chain should be STANDARD — once you've found the path, you can walk it. |
| **UNIQUE** | A window of its era | As STANDARD for the first; lower for each UNIQUE already held | Cultural identity breakthroughs. Shape your civilisation's playstyle. First civilisation to discover claims it; all others permanently locked out. Always have prerequisites — usually environmental (terrain, biome, geography) plus at least one breakthrough prereq. **Must push the player toward a playstyle** by making the prereq conditions more valuable. A UNIQUE requiring desert should make desert tiles better; one requiring mountains should reward mountain expansion. Your combination of UNIQUEs defines your civilisation's identity. |

**CORE means *visible and aimable* (v3.59).** Since CORE breakthroughs reveal themselves once eligible (§1.4), the tier is reserved for what the familiar game cannot function without. A breakthrough is CORE if it (1) unlocks a focus, or is required to make one available; (2) reveals a strategic resource; (3) grants a building or improvement every Civ V city builds — Monument, Granary, Library, Market, Temple, Walls, Aqueduct, Colosseum, Harbor, Workshop, Castle, University, Bank, Public School, Factory, Hospital, Research Lab, and the Farm, Mine, Pasture, Plantation, Quarry, Road, Railroad and Fishing Boats; (4) lies on a victory path (the Apollo Program, the spaceship parts, the United Nations, the Utopia Project) or opens the seas; (5) grants the defensive backbone — the main infantry line of each era and the first archers; (6) is a decisive experiment; or (7) is a required prerequisite of anything CORE, so that nothing CORE ever waits on an idea that may never occur. Everything else is STANDARD or rarer: specialist units, navies, air forces, wonders and flavour keep the uncertainty. The existing CORE nodes of the FTL path were left unchanged, so the endgame puzzle neither gains nor loses certainty.

**UNIQUE Scaling:** The first UNIQUE idea a civilisation can claim has the same odds as a STANDARD one. Each UNIQUE it already holds lowers the odds of the next: roughly, the second is moderately less likely, the third noticeably, the fourth quite unlikely, the fifth and later very unlikely (values for tuning; the simulator stands in a flat 6.5% for every UNIQUE). This naturally limits civilisations to 2–5 UNIQUEs depending on luck and investment, and creates strategic tension: do you pursue a fourth UNIQUE or focus those resources on advancing your existing strengths?

**UNIQUE Design Principles:**
1. **Environmental prerequisites**: Most UNIQUEs require specific terrain, biome, or geography. This ensures they naturally gravitate toward civilisations whose starting position fits.
2. **Feedback loops**: The benefits should amplify the value of whatever the prerequisites require. A desert UNIQUE should make you want *more* desert.
3. **Buildings and units**: Every UNIQUE should grant at least one special building, unit, or ability that other civilisations cannot access.
4. **No UNIQUE is required**: The game is always completable without any specific UNIQUE. They add flavour and power, not necessity.
5. **Quantity**: There should be enough UNIQUEs that every civilisation in a full game reliably discovers 2–4 that fit their situation, with many left unclaimed.

**How It Feels to the Player:**
CORE breakthroughs appear reliably — the player can count on them. STANDARD breakthroughs appear in most playthroughs; occasionally one's idea never comes, creating mild variation between games, and if other work needs it, it turns up again. UNCOMMON breakthroughs are where real divergence happens: two civs investing equally in Mechanics might find different UNCOMMON breakthroughs, leading to genuinely different technology paths. RARE breakthroughs are surprises — a player who sees one should feel lucky, and the breakthrough should be powerful enough to reward the investment that led to it.

A breakthrough can be absent from a game in only two ways, both fixed at world creation by the World-Truth ruleset derivation (§7.0): a **Removal Truth** deletes it (no gunpowder, no fossil fuels), or a **foundational axis** leaves no room for it — the branches belonging to the values this world did not draw never exist, so an elemental world has no Nuclear or Quantum Physics, a germ world no Humoral Medicine. A third group is present but may **dead-end**: the rival theories a decisive experiment adjudicates (the rival theories of heat both exist until the Joule Laboratory reports; then one validates and the other dead-ends), and frontier breakthroughs whose axis value refuses them (Nuclear Fusion can be researched in every atomic world, but in an FQ2-Impossible world it never ignites). A dead-ended breakthrough yields its consolation and its report, and obsoletes nothing. Within what a world permits, a player who meets all prerequisites always has a chance at any breakthrough, and the tiers set how good that chance is: an idea that does not come in its moment is lost to that civilisation unless diffusion brings it, rediscovery returns it (STANDARD ideas that other work waits on) or, once Revealed, an Endeavour completes it.

**Cost Multipliers (World-Truth-conditional prices):**
A breakthrough's price may carry multipliers keyed to the world's Truth values. The rule for when to use which lever: **if a breakthrough's physics is impossible in a world, remove it** (a continuum has no photon to count, so Photonics never appears there); **if it is merely harder, multiply its price** (a pocket-sized resonance computer is possible but slow to reach — Miniaturisation costs ×2.5 in Elemental worlds). A multiplier applies only to a breakthrough that exists in more than one kind of world — it expresses *how much harder the same thing is here*; a breakthrough exclusive to one world simply has its price set where it belongs. The detour margin on a Competing Solution's alternative (×1.5 while its primary exists in this world) is this lever too. The multiplier is data on the breakthrough (`costModifiers: {truth value → factor}`), evaluated once at world creation, and it is the finest balance lever the design has: a world can be made to *feel* the difficulty of a path without losing the path. Engineering rows of the FTL signature fields carry fixed multipliers (2.5×, capstones 4×; §4).

**Accelerators are head starts** (decided 14 October 2026). What a civilisation already knows can carry it partway: the Antikythera and Clockwork chains toward the Babbage Engine, the Atmospheric Engine toward the Steam Engine, Mycological Knowledge toward Antibiotics, Bio-Organic Synthesis and Biomass. The rows write these as "×0.5 (a multiplier, §1.2)"; each is applied as a **head start**, the meter starting half filled when the breakthrough comes under study, not as a lower price. Both save the same Insight, but a head start is the clearer promise ("starts half-studied" holds whenever the breakthrough opens, early or on time) and momentum favours the line from its first turn.

**Competing Solutions:**
Some goals can be reached in more than one way — furnaces hot enough for steel, power beyond a basic fission plant — and one way is usually easier or better, so most civilisations find it first and never bother with the other. The road less travelled is uncommon because it is pre-empted, not because it is hard. A **Competing Solutions group** models this: one goal, a **primary** solution and one or more **alternatives**.

- **Alternatives carry a normal tier** (usually STANDARD), so once a civilisation is on the trail it does not elude them.
- **A detour margin** — a cost multiplier on the alternative's price, ×1.5 to start — tunes the race: a civilisation pursuing both usually finds the primary first. Prerequisites shape the race too, so a civilisation that cannot reach the primary yet is already on the alternative's trail, and its study goes on every turn it stays stuck.
- **Lock-out is one-way.** Discover the primary first and the alternative is never developed (a Path Not Taken, §1.4). Discover the alternative first and the primary still arrives later, with one of two outcomes set per group: the alternative is **superseded** when the primary is better in every circumstance (its objects carry `Unavailable <after discovering [primary]>`, its units take the primary as `obsoleteTech`, its bonuses `<before discovering [primary]>`), or it **persists** when it keeps a lasting advantage — independence from a scarce resource, a capability the primary lacks, or a chain of its own. A persisting alternative can send a civilisation down a whole different road simply because it found things in a different order: the deep fission chain is the model case.
- **A persisting alternative trades; it never simply adds.** The conventional route usually wins for a reason, so an alternative carries real disadvantages as well as real advantages, and it competes for the primary's *slot* — a mutually exclusive building, the same understanding token, a bonus that does not stack. A civilisation holding both then has a choice in each city, not an accumulation: the order it found things changes its options, never its total.
- **When the primary cannot exist in this world** — removed by a Removal Truth, foreclosed by a foundational axis, or dead-ended by a frontier axis — the World-Truth ruleset derivation drops the margin and gives the alternative the primary's tier. It can never be locked out; it is simply the way.
- **Grand Endeavours suspend lock-out** (§1.5), so a civilisation can take the road less travelled on purpose.
- **A second kind of road not taken is lost confidence** rather than pre-emption: the airship chain is not superseded by aeroplanes, but each Airship Disaster lowers the tier of its undiscovered breakthroughs, for the sufferer and, by report, for its neighbours (below).
- Lock-out is always per civilisation, never global. Only alternatives in a Competing Solutions group can ever be locked out. The groups are listed in §6.

**Lost Confidence — the Airship Disaster:**
The rigid airship was not pre-empted; it was abandoned, one catastrophe at a time. The Airship Disaster models that as a second kind of road not taken — lost confidence, beside the pre-emption of Competing Solutions (above).

- **The risk.** Every gas airship — the Airship, Rigid Airship and Sky Fortress, but never the hot-air Observation Balloon — carries ⟨Triggers a [Airship Disaster] event <with [1]% chance> <upon turn start> <before discovering [Helium Extraction]>⟩. Exposure scales with investment: a lone scout rarely burns (about a 40% chance over fifty turns); a fleet of five almost surely will.
- **The disaster.** The *Airship Disaster* event (`Events.json`, a popup for the player, automatic for the AI) destroys the airship that rolled it — Unciv passes the triggering unit into the event, so its effect is ⟨[This Unit] is destroyed⟩ — and applies the confidence shock below.
- **The confidence ladder.** Each civilisation's undiscovered breakthroughs of the airship chain (AE02, AE13, AE12) stand at STANDARD, UNCOMMON or RARE. A civilisation that *suffers* a disaster drops straight to RARE. Every major civilisation in contact with it receives the *Airship Disaster Report* event and drops one step — so a first report means UNCOMMON, and a second, from any source, RARE. A civilisation that holds Helium Extraction is unshaken by reports. Confidence never recovers; a Grand Endeavour can still push a civilisation on.
- **The dynamic.** Prevalence of airships depends on whether a civilisation has sunk enough into them — or found helium — before disaster strikes, at home or abroad.

The risk and the destruction are native. The ladder and the broadcast to contacted civilisations are fork code, since tiers belong to the fork's discovery system and Unciv has no way for one civilisation's event to reach another.

**RARE Gateway Chains:**
When a RARE breakthrough serves as the gateway to a chain of related discoveries, the follow-up breakthroughs should be STANDARD (or occasionally UNCOMMON). The principle: *the hard part is finding the path; once you're on it, you should be able to walk it.* A player who gets lucky enough to find a RARE gateway shouldn't then wait on three more unlikely ideas to benefit from it.

Current RARE gateway chains:
- **Clockwork chain:** MC15 (RARE gateway) → MC16, MC17, MC18 (follow-ups)
- **Antikythera chain:** O10 (RARE gateway) → O13, O14, O15 (follow-ups)
- **Bio-computing chain:** GN08 (RARE gateway) → GN09, GN10, GN11 (follow-ups)
- **Early Steam chain:** MC07 (RARE gateway) → TH13, TH14 (follow-ups)

**Knowledge Diffusion (Catch-Up Mechanic):**

When a breakthrough becomes widespread among civilisations, it passes from cutting-edge discovery to common knowledge. This prevents runaway leads and ensures historically inevitable technologies reach everyone eventually. It counts only the civilisations a civilisation has met, and only breakthroughs it could study (their prerequisites discovered).

*After the Printing Press (MC06):* when half or more of a civilisation's contacts hold a CORE or STANDARD breakthrough, it **arrives free 10 turns later**, idea and all. The free exchange of printed ideas makes common knowledge genuinely common.

*After the Internet (CP04):* the delay falls to **5 turns**, and UNCOMMON breakthroughs arrive the same way.

*Always:* RARE and UNIQUE breakthroughs never diffuse. Seeing what a rival has, its units and buildings, reveals it (observational revelation, §1.4): diffusion's first stage, which grants nothing but the knowledge that a thing is possible.

*Proposed, not yet decided or measured:* the old design's roll bonuses, before the Printing Press and for UNCOMMON and RARE breakthroughs, become effects on ideas: when half a civilisation's contacts hold a breakthrough, its idea occurs to the civilisation, or comes back if it was lost, and the larger bonuses add a head start. Only the automatic arrivals above are in the simulator.

**Strategic Resource Access:**
Breakthroughs that require specific resources (e.g. "iron deposits in territory") can be satisfied by:
1. **Extraction:** The resource exists in your territory and you've built the relevant improvement.
2. **Trade:** You have an active trade agreement importing the resource from another civilisation. Trade-accessed resources count as "in territory" for prerequisite purposes.
3. **Conquest:** You've captured territory containing the resource.

This means a maritime civilisation with no iron mines can still research Iron Smelting if they're importing iron through trade — but at the mercy of their trade partner. Losing the trade agreement means losing access until they secure an alternative source.

### 1.3 Environmental & Circumstantial Modifiers

| Circumstance | Affected Focus Areas | Modifier |
|---|---|---|
| Coastal start | Seafaring, Trade | +30% Insight |
| River start | Farming, Trade, Construction | +20% Insight |
| Mountain/highland start | Mining, Metallurgy, Observation | +25% Insight |
| Dense forest start | Foraging, Medicine, Woodworking | +25% Insight |
| Desert/arid start | Observation, Trade, Water Management | +25% Insight |
| Island start | Seafaring | +50% Insight |
| Tundra/cold start | Fire, Animal Husbandry, Textiles | +30% Insight |
| Volcanic region | Metallurgy, Chemistry | +20% Insight |
| Diverse biome (3+ terrain types nearby) | Farming, Medicine, Foraging | +20% Insight |
| Isolated (no neighbors within X tiles) | Philosophy and the Arts, Mathematics | +15% Insight |
| Many neighbors | Warfare, Trade, Diplomacy | +20% Insight |
| Flood-prone river | Water Management, Construction | +30% Insight |
| Earthquake-prone | Construction (seismic techniques) | +25% Insight |

**Necessity Modifiers:** When a civilization faces a specific challenge (resource scarcity, military pressure, environmental stress), relevant Focus Areas gain temporary Insight bonuses. A civilization at war gains bonus Insight in Warfare. A civilization suffering famine gains bonus Insight in Farming. Necessity bonuses apply on top of the normal routing — as extra Insight to the relevant Focus regardless of which focus is active — so frontline innovation happens whether or not the state directs it.

### 1.4 Discovery States: Unknown → Revealed → Discovered

Real technological progress has two modes. Before mature theory, discovery is serendipitous — nobody set out to invent the stirrup or stumble into distillation. After theory, a second mode appears: **prediction followed by pursuit**. Fission was understood in 1938; the reactor was engineering. Fusion has been a visible target since the 1950s. The surprise mode never disappears — the transistor's inventors did not foresee the smartphone — but it stops being the only mode. This system models both.

Every breakthrough, per civilisation, is in one of three states:

**UNKNOWN** — The default. Invisible: not on any screen, not hinted at. The civilisation does not know this thing is possible. Discovery from Unknown works through ideas occurring and being studied (Section 1.2) — a surprise when it completes.

**REVEALED** — The civilisation knows this is *possible* but has not achieved it. The breakthrough appears on the research screen as a named goal with a speculative description ("our physicists believe the atom's energy could be harnessed"). Revelation is itself an event with fanfare. A Revealed breakthrough is still found the ordinary way once its idea occurs, and it can be pursued as a **Grand Endeavour** (Section 1.5) whether or not its idea has occurred.

**DISCOVERED** — Achieved. Grants its effects.

**Revelation triggers:**

1. **Theoretical revelation.** Theory-heavy breakthroughs carry a *Reveals* list: their direct applications. Discovering Atomic Model (NP02) reveals Nuclear Fission and Nuclear Weapons as concepts. Oxygen Theory (CH05) reveals the Steam Engine. Electromagnetism (EL03) reveals the Telegraph and the Generator (with its motor). Before Scientific Method (PH04), theoretical revelation is rare; after PH04, theoretical CORE/STANDARD breakthroughs reveal their applications as the norm. The game's texture therefore shifts across eras from mostly-surprise to mostly-directed — which is what happened in real history. RARE and UNIQUE breakthroughs are almost never revealed by theory: the surprises remain surprises.

2. **Proximity revelation.** Each turn a discoverable idea fails to occur, there is a chance it is glimpsed instead: the *concept* is Revealed without the idea itself — your scholars glimpse what they are circling (10% a turn for STANDARD ideas and 3% for UNCOMMON, both provisional; never RARE or UNIQUE; Section 1.2). This formalises the "promising developments" notification into a state change.

3. **Observational revelation.** Seeing another civilisation's breakthrough in effect — their steamships in your harbour, their cannon at your walls — reveals it to you. Not granted; revealed. Knowing a thing is possible is half the problem (the Soviet bomb, Meiji industrialisation). This is the first stage of Knowledge Diffusion.

4. **Espionage.** Spies can steal a *concept* (revelation) far more easily than a working breakthrough.

**The One-Step Horizon rule.** Revelation only ever reaches one step past the civilisation's frontier: a breakthrough can be Revealed only if all of its prerequisite breakthroughs are already Discovered or Revealed, and theory reveals only its *direct* applications, never transitive descendants. You can reveal mechanical computation; you cannot reveal what lies three steps beyond it. Nobody in 1950, however well-funded, glimpses the smartphone. The horizon recedes as you advance, and the far side is always dark. This rule is what preserves "seventy years ago nobody knew" for the entire game, no matter how experienced the player.

**CORE breakthroughs reveal themselves.** A CORE breakthrough becomes Revealed automatically the moment all its prerequisite breakthroughs are Discovered and its environmental prerequisites are met — the furthest reach the One-Step Horizon permits, and the one exception to the rule that revelation is rare before the Scientific Method. It gives the player back the agency to aim: a civilisation that knows Writing and Formal Logic sees the University waiting in Philosophy and the Arts, and can choose to fund it. It costs little surprise, since funding and the era premium still decide *when* it arrives, and serendipity lives in the STANDARD, UNCOMMON and RARE tiers. Because CORE now means *visible and aimable*, the tier is reserved for what a civilisation genuinely cannot do without (see the tier review, v3.59).

**Serendipitous accidents.** Ideas occur in every focus, funded or not, and the spread studies them (Section 1.2), so discoveries keep arriving in fields the civilisation is not directing. Penicillin was not the funded programme; it was the mess on the bench.

**The Paths Not Taken.** When a Revealed alternative in a Competing Solutions group is locked out before it is discovered (its primary arrives first — see *Competing Solutions*, §1.2), it remains visible as a faded entry — a thing your civilisation glimpsed and let slip. At game's end, a codex lists everything Revealed but never Discovered. The Antikythera mechanism sat at the bottom of the sea for two thousand years; that melancholy is part of the design.

### 1.5 Grand Endeavours: Directed Research

Once a breakthrough is **Revealed** and all its prerequisites are **Discovered**, the civilisation may commit to it as a **Grand Endeavour** — a directed programme that needs no idea: visible, accumulating progress, like constructing a wonder: *the fusion programme is 60% complete.*

**Rules:**
- **Under study or not.** *(v3.121; v3.120 had allowed only ideas that had not occurred.)* The usual target is a Revealed idea that has not occurred: a goal known to be possible that no one has yet had the insight for. An idea already under study can be Endeavoured too, to have it now rather than when its focus gets round to it: bomb shelters, the turn word comes that a rival has the bomb. When nothing presses, that only reorders research at twice the price: allowed to, the simulated novice and average player lose 5–6 turns of victory and the expert gains nothing (measured 15 October), so the simulated players never do it; its worth lies in the needs a game creates. It pays most for an idea trailing in a crowded focus. The Endeavour starts from nothing, as for any target, and the idea stays under study meanwhile; if study finishes it first, the Endeavour ends. *Open:* whether the Insight already spent studying the idea counts towards the Endeavour, and what becomes of the Endeavour's own Insight if study finishes first. The FTL prototype is an Endeavour of its own kind (Section 4).
- **One Endeavour at a time.** It occupies the civilisation's research focus entirely — the Endeavour *is* your focus while active.
- **Cost premium.** 2× the breakthrough's price at the moment of commitment (v3.112), filled from the active focus's share each turn: insurance, not a shortcut. RARE and UNIQUE Endeavours (formerly 4×, with the UNIQUE penalty as a further multiplier) are to be revisited; neither tier is ever glimpsed, so both are rarely Endeavour targets. Certainty is expensive; that is the trade.
- **Guaranteed completion.** An Endeavour cannot fail. If the target is an alternative in a Competing Solutions group, its lock-out is suspended while the Endeavour runs — committed pursuit keeps the road less travelled open. (Only such alternatives can ever be locked out, so every other breakthrough a world permits remains reachable indefinitely, as §1.2 promises.)
- **Spin-offs.** At 25%, 50%, and 75% progress, a small chance to reveal or discover an unrelated qualifying breakthrough — the Apollo programme effect. Big directed projects shed side-inventions.
- **Abandonment.** Progress decays slowly rather than vanishing; a resumed Endeavour picks up most of its prior work.

**Design intent.** The gamble-or-engineer decision is the same one real research funders face: wait for serendipity, or pay a premium for certainty. Endeavours must never become the default path to everything — the guards are the one-slot limit, the cost premium, the Revealed-only requirement, and the fact that RARE/UNIQUE breakthroughs almost never reveal through theory. Serendipity remains the only road to the surprises; engineering is reserved for the visible mountains. In the late game, where real civilisations do not stumble into fusion but organise toward it, Endeavours prevent the cross-focus prerequisite chains from becoming a probabilistic grind — and the final FTL launch is the greatest Endeavour of all (Section 4).

---

## 2. Eras

| Era | Approximate Real-World Analogue | Threshold |
|---|---|---|
| **Primitive** | Prehistory — Early Bronze Age | Starting era |
| **Ancient** | Bronze Age — Iron Age | 5 Breakthroughs from Ancient+ (provisional) |
| **Classical** | Classical Antiquity — Early Medieval | 3 Breakthroughs from Classical+ (provisional) |
| **Medieval** | Medieval — Early Renaissance | 3 Breakthroughs from Medieval+ (provisional) |
| **Early Modern** | Renaissance — end of the Industrial Revolution (to c. 1840) | 5 Breakthroughs from Early Modern+ (provisional) |
| **Modern** | Industrial Age — Second World War (c. 1840–1945) | 7 Breakthroughs from Modern+ (provisional) |
| **Atomic** | Nuclear and Information Ages (c. 1945–2000) | 6 Breakthroughs from Atomic+ (provisional) |
| **Space** | Near future — orbital and deep space (2000 onwards) | 4 Breakthroughs from Space+ (provisional) |
| **Interstellar** | FTL and Beyond | Endgame |

**Era entry — provisional, a lever for playtesting.** A civilisation enters an era when it holds the number of breakthroughs above from that era or later. Entering an era unlocks the policy branches tied to it, applies the era's own uniques (some reach the whole game, such as a spy for every major civilisation once anyone enters a given era), raises Faith purchase prices, and changes embarked units' defence, research agreements' cost and the AI's valuation of cities. The fork's costs, cost scaling and discovery pricing do not depend on it: they follow the era of the row, or the civilisation's development. Base Civ V enters an era with its first technology from it, which suits a dense chain of techs whose leading edge is never far ahead of the rest. Here a single breakthrough can arrive well ahead of its era — through a specialist, or luck — and under that rule a Mathematics specialist would enter the Early Modern to Atomic eras 0.8–1.1 eras early (up to 1.7), firing game-wide triggers early for everyone. The counts are about a tenth of each era's expected discoveries, so a thin era needs fewer: a balanced civilisation then enters within about 0.15 era of its development, the thin Classical era about a third of an era late. They were measured on the rate-model candidate in the discovery workshop, so they depend on the discovery model's pace and must be confirmed once that is chosen. The previous counts (8, 12, 15, 18, 20, 18, 15) dated from the first draft and left entry up to an era behind development.

**Era boundaries.** Each era is a span of the Insight curve: a civilisation's development is in an era while its cumulative Insight lies inside the span. Up to the Early Modern era, an era's discovery price is its span ÷ its expected discoveries (§1.2), so each discovery costs the same share of the era, and the spans are set so that discoveries come at an even pace (v3.108). Two exceptions smooth the opening (v3.123). The Ancient era, whose many small breakthroughs had come faster than one a turn, is priced 1.5× its even-pace price, with its span to match. The Primitive span is 1.3× its expected discoveries' worth at its old price: civilisations are still finishing Primitive breakthroughs when its Insight runs out, and the longer span keeps development's entry to the Ancient era within 6–9 turns of their entry by the count above, as before, rather than 10–14. The last three eras follow base Civ V's own timing instead (v3.122, provisional): a good player's development enters the Atomic era around turn 228 and the Space era around turn 264, as base Civ V's does and about when civilisations enter them by the count above, and leaves the Space era around turn 320. Their prices are their v3.108 ones, set by the design's hardest constraint: **a science victory takes as long as base Civ V's for the same player on the same economy**, within five turns either way, because domination, cultural and diplomatic victories are unchanged and balanced against base Civ V's game length. The reference is base Civ V's own tree, simulated to a launched spaceship on the same income: turn 305 on a good economy and 380 on an average one. (v3.122's dearer late prices, ×1.25 in the Modern and Atomic eras and ×2 in the Space era, made it 22–32 turns longer and were withdrawn in v3.123.) Base Civ V's own eras, its tech-column costs, are shown for comparison; the fork's no longer coincide with them.

| Era | Insight span | Ends at | Turns (good player) | Price per discovery | Base Civ V's span |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Primitive** | 194 | 194 | 33 | 5 | 160 |
| **Ancient** | 858 | 1,052 | 36 | 22 (×1.5) | 385 |
| **Classical** | 1,538 | 2,590 | 28 | 65 | 1,275 |
| **Medieval** | 4,269 | 6,859 | 36 | 144 | 3,900 |
| **Early Modern** | 17,041 | 23,900 | 49 | 439 | 19,250 |
| **Modern** | 42,331 | 66,231 | 46 | 951 | 41,300 |
| **Atomic** | 47,398 | 113,629 | 36 | 1,369 | 46,000 |
| **Space** | 96,047 | 209,676 | 56 | 1,399 | 90,200 |

**The late eras (v3.122–v3.123, provisional; to be confirmed in play).** Until v3.122 development entered the Space era around turn 285 on a good economy, 24–29 turns after civilisations entered it by count, so the FTL race was bought at the ahead-of-time premium and finished before development reached the Space era. Now the Space era's span covers the race, from about when civilisations reach the unified theory to about when a good player wins. Measured on the full design (v3.123: all 15 victory conditions, 2,700 games with rivals at the same prices, none unfinished), the expert wins at a median of turn 304, the novice 307 and the average player 380, against base Civ V's 305, 305 and 380; civilisations enter every era by count within 9 turns of their development reaching it. The cost is the late pace. A game holds about as many discoveries as turns whatever the prices, since players discover nearly everything that occurs to them and prices only set how long it takes; and the Modern to Space breakthroughs take 85% of the Insight spent on discoveries, so they cannot get dearer without lengthening the science victory. So from the Modern era a good economy makes about 1.1–1.4 discoveries a turn counting rivals' knowledge. Fewer late discoveries would need fewer late breakthroughs, which is under review.

| F02 | **Kiln Construction** | CORE | Primitive | F01 | **Shrine** (and the Maya **Pyramid** it is replaced by). ⟨[+25]% City Strength from defensive buildings⟩ — walls of fired brick. Kiln heat — fired pots, glazes, fired brick — leads on to Glassmaking (OP01) and Geopolymer Concrete (S09). | |
| F03 | **Charcoal Production** | CORE | Primitive | F01 | ⟨[+25]% Production when constructing [Melee] units [in all cities]⟩ — hotter fires, harder blades. The hotter fire that makes smelting (F04) and iron (ME04) possible. Wood tar, the kiln's by-product: ⟨[-25]% maintenance on road & railroads⟩; ⟨[+10]% Strength <for [Water] units>⟩ — tarred roads, caulked hulls (Pitch and Tar, merged here in v3.101). | Key bridge to Metallurgy. |
| F04 | **Smelting Discovery** | CORE | Primitive | F03 + S04 (Ore Identification) | *Unlocks* **Metallurgy** focus. ⟨[+1 Production] from [Copper] tiles [in all cities]⟩ and ⟨[+1 Gold] from [Gold Ore] tiles [in all cities]⟩ — the ores Ore Identification (S04) revealed become worth working. | The moment someone puts green rock near hot charcoal. Transformative. |
| F05 | **Signal Fires** | STANDARD | Ancient | F01 + SO03 (Tribal Law) | **Signal Fire**ⁿ [improvement on Hill; 5 turns; ⟨[+1] Sight⟩; ⟨Gives a defensive bonus of [20]%⟩]. An early-warning post on high ground. |  |
| F06 | **Greek Fire** | UNCOMMON | Classical | F03 (Charcoal Production — wood tar) + O08 (Geological Observation — sulphur) | **Fire Ship**ⁿ [unit, Melee Water; cost 70; mv 4; str 14; ⟨[+50]% Strength <when attacking>⟩; ⟨[+50]% Strength <vs [Water] units>⟩]; ⟨[+25]% Strength <for [Siege] units> <vs cities> <when attacking>⟩. Pine pitch, sulfur and a siphon — no petroleum required. *Also discoverable through Warfare or Seafaring focus.* | Historical Byzantine weapon. |
| F07 | **Advanced Pyrotechnics** | CORE | Medieval | F03 + O08 (Geological Observation — sulphur) + MI08 (Saltpeter Mining — the oxidiser) | **Fireworks Ground**ⁿ [70/1; +1 Culture, +1 Happiness; requires **Saltpeter**]. Prerequisite for Gunpowder (WA10) unless GUNPOWDER_ABSENT. |  |
| F09 | **Controlled Burn Agriculture** | UNCOMMON | Primitive | F01 + G02 (Seed Saving) | ⟨[-50]% construction time for [Remove Forest] improvements⟩ and ⟨[-50]% construction time for [Remove Jungle] improvements⟩; ⟨[+25]% Yield from pillaging tiles⟩ (burning what you clear). Land is opened fast — and what is burnt gives back. |  |
| F10 | **Thermal Lance** | RARE | Ancient | F01 + F03 + ME-B (Bellows) | ⟨[+30]% Production when constructing [All] wonders [in all cities]⟩. Stone shaped by directed heat. | Speculative: what if directed-heat tools were developed early? |
| F11 | **Volcanic Forge Tradition** | UNIQUE | Classical | F03 + ME-B (Bellows) + Volcanic region | **Volcanic Forge**ⁿ [replaces Forge; 120/1; +2 Production; ⟨[+1 Production] from [Volcanic] tiles [in this city]⟩; ⟨[+15]% Production when constructing [Land] units [in this city]⟩; ⟨Must have an owned [Volcanic] within [2] tiles⟩]. Civ unique: ⟨[+1 Production] from [Volcanic] tiles [in all cities]⟩ — the more vents you hold, the more you forge. | Icelandic/Japanese tradition of forging with Earth's own heat. |
| F15 | **Signal Fire Network** | UNIQUE | Ancient | F05 (Signal Fires) + WA06 (Fortification Design) + Mountain/highland or Many neighbors biome | **Signal Tower**ⁿ [20/0; ⟨[+10]% Strength for cities⟩ (applies to this city)]. Civ unique: ⟨[+20]% Strength <for [Military] units> <when defending> <in [Hill] tiles>⟩ — the high ground warns and holds. | Greek phryctoriae, Chinese beacon towers, Gondor. Relocated from the former Communications focus. |
| F16 | **Turbary** | STANDARD | Ancient | F03 (Charcoal Production) + Marsh in territory | **Peat Cutting**ⁿ (improvement; `terrainsCanBeBuiltOn: [Marsh]`; 6 turns; ⟨[+2 Production]⟩; ⟨Does not need removal of [Marsh]⟩) — fuel burned where it lies. A bog can now be drained (the Aqueduct's flood control, WM02) or cut. A worked Peat Cutting also counts as a fuel tile for a Charcoal Kiln (ME08), and lets a city's Distillery make Whisky (CH04). Needs a Marsh in the civilisation's territory: a people with no bogs never learns to burn them. (Named apart from its improvement under the filter discipline of §7.0, since the kiln and the Distillery both filter on `[Peat Cutting]`.) | Pliny described the Chauci burning peat in the first century; the right to cut it, *turbary*, is one of the traditional common rights of English law. |

| O12 | **Atmospheric Science** | STANDARD | Modern | O02 + CH05 (Oxygen Theory) + MA05 (Calculus) | ⟨[+1 Gold] from all [Harbor] buildings⟩ — storm warnings for shipping. Prerequisite for Planetary Engineering (SE07). (The empire-wide farm bonus belongs to Catalysis, CH12 — the Haber process.) | |

### FOCUS: MINING ⛰️
*Available from: S04 (Ore Identification)*

*Also reachable in this focus (dual-path; home focus in parentheses): Explosives / non-gunpowder (CH08, from Chemistry).*

**Resource System Note:** Surface mineral deposits (copper, tin, gold, iron, gems) are revealed by S04 (Ore Identification) and become extractable through F04 (Smelting — for soft metals) and ME04 (Iron Smelting — for iron). The Mining focus is about going deeper, finding strategic resources, and improving extraction efficiency.

| # | Breakthrough | Cat. | Era | Prerequisites | Unlocks | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| MI05 | **Deep Shaft Mining** | CORE | Ancient | S04 + CO01 (Mud Brick — shaft support) | *Reveals* **Silver** and **Salt** (deep deposits — the second reveal); ⟨[+1 Production] from [Mine] tiles [in all cities]⟩. Going underground changes everything. | Going underground changes everything. |
| MI06 | **Coal Discovery** | CORE | Medieval | MI05 | *Reveals* **Coal** (the canonical Industrialization `revealedBy`); ⟨[+1 Production] from [Coal] tiles [in all cities]⟩. **Absent in FOSSIL_FUELS_ABSENT worlds**, where there is no coal to discover: the Removal Truth gives Coal ⟨Doesn't generate naturally⟩, and a discovery with nothing to find does not exist. Charcoal and renewable paths substitute. | Prerequisite for Industrial revolution paths. |
| MI06B | **Volcanic Ash Processing** | STANDARD | Ancient | S04 + Volcanic region | **Pozzolana Works**ⁿ [20/0; cityHealth +10; ⟨[-25]% maintenance cost for [All] buildings [in this city]⟩; ⟨Must have an owned [Volcanic] within [2] tiles⟩]; ⟨[+1 Food] from [Volcanic] tiles [in all cities]⟩ (fertile ash). Prerequisite for Roman Concrete (CO04). |  |
| MI07 | **Petroleum Discovery** | CORE | Early Modern | MI05 + O08 (Geological Observation) + MC08 (Steam Engine — the steam-driven drilling rig, and an industry that wanted lamp oil) | *Reveals* **Oil** (the canonical Biology `revealedBy`); **Oil well**, **Offshore Platform** (improvements); ⟨[+1 Gold] from [Oil] tiles [in all cities]⟩ (kerosene). **Absent in FOSSIL_FUELS_ABSENT worlds**, where there is no petroleum to discover: the Removal Truth gives Oil ⟨Doesn't generate naturally⟩, and a discovery with nothing to find does not exist. Bio-organic chemistry and alcohol engines substitute. |  |
| MI08 | **Saltpeter Mining** | CORE | Medieval | MI05 + CH01 (Alchemy — saltpetre was an alchemist's find) | *Reveals* **Saltpeter**ⁿ (new strategic resource; on Desert, Plains and Hill tiles; improved by Mine; deposits of 2–4, like Iron); ⟨[+1] Unit Supply per city⟩ (preserved rations). **The gunpowder strategic — consumed by** the Musketman (WA10), the Cannon (WA22) and the Fireworks Ground (F07); the Air Rifleman (WA18) pointedly needs none. A civilisation in a gunpowder world without nitre fights with pikes and crossbows until Rifling (WA16), exactly as an iron-less civilisation fights with Pikemen — and trades or marches for it meanwhile. Valuable even in GUNPOWDER_ABSENT worlds. | Valuable for food/military supply even in GUNPOWDER_ABSENT games. |

The late-game high-tech strategic Civ V never had: **consumed by** the Microchip Foundry (EN03), the Fab (EN06), the Drone (WA20) and the Solar Farm (ES07), and a prerequisite for Miniaturisation (EN10). A civilisation without it cannot build the electronics line — or must trade, conquer, or (Substance worlds) transmute for it. |  |
| MI12 | **Asteroid Prospecting** | STANDARD | Space | SE02 (Orbital Assembly) + MI05 | **Asteroid Mining Station**ⁿ [940/5; ⟨Provides [2] [Iron]⟩; ⟨Provides [2] [Aluminum]⟩; ⟨Provides [2] [Aluminum] <after discovering [Orbital Resource Delivery]>⟩; ⟨Provides [2] [Rare Earths] <after discovering [Orbital Resource Delivery]>⟩; ⟨Limited to [3] per Civilization⟩] — the Recycling Center pattern: strategic resources from orbit end scarcity. | |
| MI13 | **Wood Gas Production** | STANDARD | Early Modern | F03 (Charcoal) + CH05 (Oxygen Theory) | ⟨[-25]% construction time for [Lumber mill] improvements⟩ — timber becomes fuel. **Strategically critical in FOSSIL_FUELS_ABSENT worlds.** *Also discoverable through Chemistry.* | Historical: wood gas vehicles were widespread in WWII fuel-scarce Europe. |

Civ unique: ⟨[+1 Production] from [{Mine} {Hill}] tiles [in all cities]⟩ — the mountain's mines are sacred, and they pay. | Cornish/Saxon/Andean mining traditions where mining was a sacred craft. |

| ME08 | **Blast Furnace** | CORE | Medieval | ME04 + CO02 (Post and Lintel) + (WM05 or MC04 — bellows driven by a mill) + (MI06 or F03) — Coal Discovery, or Charcoal Production | **Ironworks** (national wonder). Requires **Coal**. **Charcoal Kiln**ⁿ [210/2; ⟨Provides [1] [Coal] <in cities without a [Distillery]> <when number of [Worked [non-[{non-[Forest]} {non-[Jungle]} {non-[Peat Cutting]}]] Tiles in this city] is more than [2]>⟩ and ⟨Provides [1] [Coal] <in cities with a [Distillery]> <when number of [[Worked [non-[{non-[Forest]} {non-[Jungle]} {non-[Peat Cutting]}]] Tiles in this city] - min([Worked [Peat Cutting] Tiles in this city], 1)] is more than [2]>⟩; ⟨[-10]% [Production] [in this city] <in cities without a [Distillery]> <when number of [Worked [non-[{non-[Forest]} {non-[Jungle]} {non-[Peat Cutting]}]] Tiles in this city] is more than [2]>⟩ and ⟨[-10]% [Production] [in this city] <in cities with a [Distillery]> <when number of [[Worked [non-[{non-[Forest]} {non-[Jungle]} {non-[Peat Cutting]}]] Tiles in this city] - min([Worked [Peat Cutting] Tiles in this city], 1)] is more than [2]>⟩ — in a city with a Distillery, one worked Peat Cutting is smoking malt and does not count as fuel (`min(…, 1)`, so a Distillery claims a bog only if there is one to claim)] — lit only while its city works at least three fuel tiles: forest, jungle or a Peat Cutting. Unworked woods feed nothing, and a cold kiln costs nothing. Charcoal counts as Coal, so it fuels a Factory or an Ironclad like any other; the −10% cancels a Factory's own +10%, so a kiln-fed Factory is worth only its flat +4 at any size — charcoal does not scale with industry, which is why coal won. Either fuel opens the furnace — coal where there are seams, charcoal where there are none — and every civilisation can raise kilns: an expensive way to get Coal, worth it to a civilisation short of seams, or to one pushing to raise a coal-powered army larger than its seams could supply. | In our world, Sweden ran charcoal blast furnaces into the 19th century, and England's iron was charcoal-smelted until coke took over in the 1700s. |

| ME12 | **Bessemer Process** | CORE | Modern | ME08 + CH05 (Oxygen Theory) | **Battleship** (unit — steel hulls); ⟨[+1 Production] from [Iron] tiles [in all cities]⟩ (cheap steel). Prerequisite for Railway Network (CO23) and Reinforced Concrete (CO11). |  |

| ME14 | **Titanium Working** | UNCOMMON | Atomic | ME12 + CH06 (Periodic Table) | **Modern Armor** (unit); ⟨[+10]% Strength <for [Armored] units>⟩. |  |
| ME15 | **Memory Alloys** | RARE | Atomic | ME14 + MS01 (Composite Materials) | ⟨[-33]% maintenance cost for [All] buildings [in all cities]⟩ (structures that repair themselves); all Military units gain the **Adaptive Armour**ⁿ promotion (⟨[+10]% Strength <when defending>⟩). |  |

### FOCUS: PHILOSOPHY AND THE ARTS 🎭
*Available from: SO02 (Storytelling) + SO03 (Tribal Law)*

**The institutions of mind and spirit — faith, learning and art.** Merged in v3.58 from Philosophy and Religion & Culture, the two thinnest early foci, so that the Temple, the University and the Scientific Method sit where a player funding thought and faith would look for them — as in Civ V, where Philosophy brings the Temple and Education the University — and so that the focus offers something in every era from the Primitive to the Modern. Breakthrough IDs keep their PH and CU prefixes; nothing that cites them changes.

| CU12 | **Sacred Dance Tradition** | UNIQUE | Primitive | CU04 (Musical Instruments) + SO02 + Tropical or Desert biome | **Dance Ground**ⁿ [replaces Monument; 40/1; +2 Culture, +1 Faith, +1 Happiness]. Civ unique: ⟨[+1 Happiness] [in all cities] <in cities with a [Dance Ground]>⟩ — cohesion in every city that dances. | West African, Aboriginal Australian, Native American ceremonial dance. |
| CU13 | **Talking Drum Networks** | UNIQUE | Primitive | SO02 + Dense forest/jungle biome | **Drum Tower**ⁿ [10/0; +1 Culture; ⟨Must have an owned [Forest] within [2] tiles⟩]. Civ unique: ⟨Resting point for Influence with City-States is increased by [10]⟩ — every neighbour hears the drums. | West African-inspired. Relocated from the former Communications focus. |

Choose each city's temperament — a governance lever no germ world has. | The body politic, literally balanced. |
| MD20 | **Phage Therapy** | STANDARD | Modern | MD05 (Germ Theory validated) + OP03 (Telescope — the microscope) | **Phage Institute**ⁿ [250/1; requires a **Hospital** in this city; ⟨[+15]% growth [in this city]⟩; ⟨[15]% Food is carried over after population increases [in this city]⟩; ⟨Remove [Medical Lab] [in this city]⟩] — a library of bacteriophages, each matched to one strain. Grants MASS_MEDICINE (the phage route). **The trade:** no empire-wide effect — antibiotics are broad, cheap and mass-produced, and that is why they usually win; phages must be matched city by city, and an Institute occupies the slot a Medical Lab would. What phages keep is a future: engineered, they grow stronger (MD21). **Competing Solution** (§1.2): the alternative to Antibiotics (MD06), detour margin ×1.5 while Antibiotics exists in this world; if Antibiotics is discovered first this is never developed, and if it arrives later this *persists* — a city chooses between a Phage Institute and a Medical Lab, never both. Germ worlds only (FQ10). | Félix d'Hérelle was treating infections with phages in the 1920s; the Eliava Institute in Tbilisi kept the practice alive after the West chose penicillin, and resistance has brought it back. |
| MD21 | **Engineered Phages** | STANDARD | Space | MD20 + GN05 (Synthetic Biology) | ⟨[+15]% growth [in all cities] <in cities with a [Phage Institute]>⟩ — phages designed rather than found: the Institutes double in worth. The late payoff for the harder road, and only for cities that took it. Germ worlds only. | |

*Available from: MA01 (Geometry) + S03 (Quarrying)*

|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|

Gears turn everything that follows; the Workshop comes with the crank that turns their motion to work (MC19). ⟨[-25]% construction time for [Farm] improvements⟩ — the seed drill is a geared machine, Han-era in China (Mechanical Seed Drill, merged here in v3.101). ⟨[+10]% Production when constructing [All] wonders [in all cities]⟩ — the crane lifts what the arm cannot: lever, pulley and gear together, as in Hellenistic mechanics (Lever and Pulley, merged here in v3.102). | Toothed gears are Hellenistic and Han: Philo's geared water wheel (3rd c. BC), the Antikythera mechanism (2nd c. BC), Vitruvius's mill and hodometer; the lever and pulley are older (Classical since v3.119). |
| MC04 | **Windmill** | CORE | Medieval | MC03 (Gearing) + TX01 (Weaving — the sails' cloth) | **Windmill** — the mill for country with no river to turn one. Where there is no Water Mill, it drives the industrial chain in its place: the crank (MC19), the Blast Furnace's bellows, Plate Armour's hammers and the Steam Engine's gearing. *Also discoverable through Energy Systems.* | CORE (v3.117) as the Water Mill's alternative for a civilisation without a river or water source. Sistan's vertical-axis mills (10th c.) needed no gears; the European post mill (1185) took the water mill's; the Dutch wind sawmill of the 1590s put a crankshaft on one. |

Switches Knowledge Diffusion to its post-press phase (Section 1.2). The most important breakthrough in the game. | Most important breakthrough in the game. |

Prerequisite for the Railway (CO23). Costs ×0.5 if the Atmospheric Engine (TH14) is known (a multiplier, §1.2). | Watt's separate condenser (1769) began as a repair to a model Newcomen engine, hence the Atmospheric Engine's discount. Where no Atmospheric Engine came first, Newcomen's engine is folded in here — and it ran for decades before oxygen chemistry existed, so this needs the Scientific Method, not Oxygen Theory. |
| MC09 | **Internal Combustion Engine** | CORE | Modern | MC08 + ((MI07 + CH09) or (CH04 + CH07)) | **Landship**, **Tank**, **Anti-Tank Gun** (units — Tank requires **Oil**; the Landship and Tank take this row as their `requiredTech`). **Distillery Fuel Depot**ⁿ [375/2; requires a **Distillery** in this city; ⟨Provides [1] [Oil] <when number of [Worked [Farm] Tiles in this city] is more than [2]>⟩; ⟨[-20]% growth [in this city] <when number of [Worked [Farm] Tiles in this city] is more than [2]>⟩] — grain into spirit, only while its city works at least three Farms; ethanol counts as Oil, so it fuels any tank, battleship or aircraft, and its cost scales with the surplus the grain would have become. Either fuel opens the engine — petroleum refined where there are wells (Petroleum Discovery and Petrochemistry), distilled alcohol where there are none (Distillation and Organic Chemistry) — and every civilisation can raise Depots: an expensive way to get Oil, worth it to a civilisation short of wells, or to one pushing to field more tanks and aircraft than its wells could fuel. *Also discoverable through Chemistry.* | Brazil ran its economy on ethanol, and fuel-starved Europe ran its cars on alcohol and wood gas in the Second World War. |

Savery sold it as *The Miner's Friend*, but its lift was too short for mines; it found its work in houses and gardens. |

Stationary, reciprocating and ruinous on fuel, so it burns Coal where Coal is cheap — from a pit, or from a Charcoal Kiln. Makes the Steam Engine (MC08) cost ×0.5 (a multiplier, §1.2). **Competing Solution** (§1.2): the alternative to Steam Engine (MC08), detour margin ×1.5 while Steam Engine exists in this world; if Steam Engine is discovered first this is never developed, and if it arrives later this is *superseded* — no new Engine Houses, but existing ones keep pumping until converted: a Steam Works in their city goes up twice as fast, reusing the house, beam and pit, and retires them. Converting frees the Coal and gains the Steam Works' power, but gives up the mine and mill yields, so a dry mining town may keep its old engine for a while. Follow-up of the Early Steam chain (§1.2). | Newcomen's engine (1712) never ran above about one atmosphere: the air did the work, and a piston sealed with leather and a layer of water hid a poor bore, so it needed no machining. It paid only at the pithead, burning coal no one could sell, and in the mid-1700s engines pumped water back over wheels to run mills. Newcomen engines kept working for decades after Watt's arrived, and many were refitted with Watt's condenser rather than replaced. |

### FOCUS: CHEMISTRY 🧪
*Available from: ME01 (Copper Working) + O06 (Natural Philosophy), or F03 (Charcoal) + ME04 (Iron Smelting)*

*Also reachable in this focus (dual-path; home focus in parentheses): Wood Gas Production (MI13, from Mining); Internal Combustion Engine (MC09, from Mechanics); Biomass/Biogas (ES13, from Energy Systems); Gunpowder (WA10, from Warfare); Phase Exchange (TR16, from Trade).*

It needs a *worked* Peat Cutting in the distilling city — the peat is cut and burned — so every such city makes one, to enjoy or to trade. That peat is spent: a distilling city counts one fewer worked Peat Cutting toward its Charcoal Kiln's fuel (ME08). The mineral acids were themselves products of distillation — vitriol distilled into oil of vitriol, saltpetre and alum into *aqua fortis* — so the chemistry and electricity that need acids follow this row (Acid/Base Discovery, merged here in v3.105). Prerequisite for Oxygen Theory (CH05), Explosives (CH08) and Electricity (EL02). | |
